PQC Today capability coverage matrix

PQC Today executes selected public reference vectors, standards tests, conformance cases, and implementation probes. A passing result is evidence only for the identified test, operation, parameters, implementation build, and target. It is not an ACVTS verdict, a CAVP/CMVP certificate, or proof of exhaustive conformance.

Generated from the runtime mechanism inventory, the reviewed capability map and the registered test inventory. A status says a registered test exists for the cell; it is not a pass. Machine-readable copy: coverage-matrix.json.

Headline

  • C++: 1635 advertised capability cells (165 mechanisms). Positive: 184 covered, 1254 sampled, 197 untested. Negative: 78 covered, 63 sampled, 1494 untested. 31 further cells unsupported. WebAssembly runs: 1552 cells with a recorded pass, 9 with a recorded fail, 0 with a recorded skip (not run, never a pass).
  • Rust: 1028 advertised capability cells (174 mechanisms). Positive: 183 covered, 678 sampled, 167 untested. Negative: 75 covered, 47 sampled, 906 untested. 638 further cells unsupported. WebAssembly runs: 967 cells with a recorded pass, 6 with a recorded fail, 0 with a recorded skip (not run, never a pass).

Rules and definitions

cell
A capability cell is one (mechanism, operation, parameter set, sign variant). Mechanism, operation (from CKF_ flags) and key-size range are DISCOVERED from the running engine (C_GetMechanismList + C_GetMechanismInfo); parameter sets, sign variants and boundaries are DECLARED in capability-map.json and kept only when the parameter set falls inside the engine-reported key-size range.
denominator
Denominator (per engine) = advertised capability cells: the engine lists the mechanism, sets the CKF_ flag for the operation, and the declared parameter set falls inside its reported key-size range. Cells the engine does not advertise, and declared capabilities no PKCS #11 mechanism can express, are counted separately as unsupported and shown — never dropped.
status
Cell status = the strongest evidence class among registered test cases that exercise the cell on that engine (order: acvts-issued > nist-reference > standard-kat > oracle > differential > round-trip > behavior-only). No registered case = untested. Not advertised = unsupported. A status says a test EXISTS; it is not a pass.
covered
Covered (per polarity) = at least 2 distinct registered cases of that polarity exercise the cell AND, for positive, at least one of them checks an externally expected value (acvts-issued, nist-reference, standard-kat or oracle). A cell is covered overall only when positive, negative, boundary and state-error are all covered.
sampled
Sampled = at least one registered case, but the covered rule is not met. One happy-path case is always "sampled", never "covered" (plan Q5).
polarity
Polarities are counted separately: a positive case never counts toward negative, boundary or state-error. Boundary = a registered case whose recorded parameters hit a declared boundary of the cell (e.g. context length 0 or 255, empty message); it may also be a positive or negative case. State-error = a case asserting a PKCS #11 state or argument error.
parity
Parity = both engines have a RECORDED PASS of the same registered case on the same artifact kind. Skipped, unsupported and not-run never count as a pass, and a recorded result counts only for the exact artifact (sha256) the mechanism inventory records. Rows with no such pair are "not established".
artifacts
Artifact kinds are reported separately: wasm (the two shipped WebAssembly engines — registered tests run in the browser), native, hardware. Native and hardware targets have not been run: they are "not run" and never counted as pass.
dimensions
Plan G-3 — two further columns per cell, from the same registered cases. Algorithm correctness = cases whose expected value is checked (acvts-issued, nist-reference, standard-kat, oracle, differential, round-trip; state-error cases excluded); covered when at least 2 distinct cases exist and one has an externally expected value. PKCS #11 API behaviour = cases that assert only PKCS #11 behaviour (behavior-only evidence: return codes, operation state, buffers, attributes) plus every state-error case; covered when at least 2 distinct cases exist and one is a state-error case. Neither column says a test passed.
inventory
Per engine and artifact kind the matrix counts, separately: advertised cells (the denominator — C_GetMechanismList × operation × parameter set × variant), registered cells (≥ 1 registered case, by evidence status), cells with a recorded pass, with a recorded fail, and with a recorded skip (the engine did not advertise a mechanism the case needs, so it was not run), unsupported cells (not advertised) and untested cells (advertised, no registered case). A skip is never a pass and is never folded into untested.
numerator
Numerators (per engine, per polarity) = advertised cells whose level is covered, sampled or untested under the rules below; covered + sampled + untested = the denominator. Per-status numerators count advertised cells by their strongest registered evidence class.

Artifact kinds and parity

  • wasm — registered; partly recorded. Registered tests target the shipped WebAssembly engines. Recorded results: 4432 from errorPathProbes (every registered error-path case, dual engine) (Node.js via vitest local venue (not a browser), 2026-09-26, hub 982e64c62); 94 from katRunner (every registered KatKind) (Node.js via vitest local venue (not a browser), 2026-09-27, hub 0f960cc94); 7022 from useAcvpSuite (all categories, dual engine) (Node.js via vitest local venue (not a browser), 2026-09-26, hub 982e64c62). Tests without a recorded result are never reported as passed.
  • native — not-run. macOS, Linux x86-64 and Linux Arm64 native builds have not been run through these tests (plan H-2). Never counted as pass.
  • hardware — not-run. i.MX 95 and KV260 targets have not been run through these tests (plan H-3). Never counted as pass.

106 C++/Rust C_GetMechanismInfo disagreementsopenunassignedG-9C++ vs Rust (wasm)—Triaged 2026-09-25 against pqctoday-hsm @ a22e6ca0 (post PRs #255/#257-260): 106 mechanisms differ across 144 (mechanism, field) pairs. Top actionable item: 52 of the 58 bugs share one root cause - Rust's C_MessageSignInit already delegates to C_SignInit and works for RSA-PKCS-sign (20), CKM_HASH_ML_DSA* (11), CKM_HASH_SLH_DSA* (11) and CKM_ECDSA* (10), but the mechanism_info arms in rust/src/ffi.rs (~1481, ~1628-1648, ~1201) were never updated to advertise CKF_MESSAGE_SIGN|CKF_MESSAGE_VERIFY, unlike cpp's already-set precedent (SoftHSM_slots.cpp, 'audit mech G2'). None of this is fixed yet - awaiting a decision on whether to open a follow-up PR.

ECDSA P-521 NIST sample failed on C++ — hub CKA_EC_POINT encoding (fixed hub-side)in-progressunassignedE (ECDSA) / J-7C++ (wasm) — hub adapter—Root cause (triage 2026-09-24): hsm_importECPublicKey wrote the DER OCTET STRING length of the 133-byte P-521 point as the single byte 0x85, which DER reads as '5 length octets follow'; the C++ engine parsed a garbage CKA_EC_POINT and rejected the NIST ACVP-Server ECDSA-SigVer-FIPS186-5 sample (workbench §33), while Rust passed because it reads the raw CKA_VALUE copy. Hub adapter bug, not an engine defect. Fixed by the X.690 long-form length (softhsm/helpers derOctetString); the re-recorded run shows the case passing on both engines. Kept on record until the release freeze re-captures evidence on the browser host.
SUCI key-injection helper is rejected by the C++ engineopenunassignedA-3C++ (wasm) — hub helper—hsm_injectTestKey unwraps the raw EC scalar with C_UnwrapKeyAuthenticated using a template that carries CKA_EC_PARAMS; the C++ engine rejects it with CKR_ATTRIBUTE_READ_ONLY (the same unwrap without CKA_EC_PARAMS succeeds). katRunner suci-profile-b steps 1-2 (import only, not registered) therefore error on C++; steps 3-7 import with C_CreateObject and pass on both engines. The KAT surfaces run the Rust engine. Hub helper limitation.
PKCS #11 v3.2 gives no single key-handle / key-type code for C_DecapsulateKey — not probedopenunassignedG-8C++ + Rust — specification—§5.18.9 lists CKR_UNWRAPPING_KEY_HANDLE_INVALID and CKR_UNWRAPPING_KEY_TYPE_INCONSISTENT (not CKR_KEY_HANDLE_INVALID / CKR_KEY_TYPE_INCONSISTENT), while §5.1.6 says the UNWRAPPING_* codes "can only be returned by C_UnwrapKey". With no single specified value there is no key-handle or key-type probe for C_DecapsulateKey (the engines differ: C++ returns CKR_UNWRAPPING_KEY_HANDLE_INVALID for handle 0, Rust CKR_KEY_HANDLE_INVALID). Likewise C_WrapKey, C_UnwrapKey, C_DeriveKey and C_EncapsulateKey require the usage attribute but do not list CKR_KEY_FUNCTION_NOT_PERMITTED, so no key-usage probe exists for them. Specification ambiguity, not an engine defect. Recorded by the G-8 error-path probes (errorPathCatalog.ts / errorPathProbes.ts) in run-results/wasm-node-errorPathProbes.json; the rows stay failing — the assertion is the PKCS #11 v3.2 value and is not loosened.
Classical NIST reference samples cover only what the pinned upstream samples registeropenunassignedEC++ + Rust—The pinned ACVP-Server@975de31e samples bound what WS-E can claim: ACVP-AES-GCM-1.0 registers AES-128 only (IV 96/120, tag 128/32, payload/AAD 0/120 bits) — AES-192/256-GCM, 64-bit tags and other lengths have no NIST case; HMAC 2.0 registers macLen 80..160 bits — no full-length (untruncated) tag, so CKM_<hash>_HMAC (non-general) has no NIST case; EDDSA-SigVer-1.0 carries no context string (contexts are exercised only through the EDDSA-SigGen-1.0 byte-match cases of eddsa_siggen_acvp_test); ECDSA SHA2-512/256 and SHAKE groups have no PKCS#11 v3.2 CKM_ECDSA_<hash> mechanism (skip rows). Checked again 2026-09-25 (GitHub API, gap-closure P5): 975de31e (2026-08-12, "RELEASE/v1.1.0.43 including hotfix patches") is still the head of usnistgov/ACVP-Server master and the only other branch (jbrock24-patch-1, 2024) changes the README only, so no newer upstream commit carries AES-192/256-GCM or full-length HMAC tag samples; the scope gap stays.
Classical families still without NIST ACVP-Server cases after WS-E and gap-closure P5openunassignedEC++ + Rust—Closed by gap-closure P5 (2026-09-25): HKDF (hkdf_acvp_test, KDA-HKDF-Sp800-56Cr2), SP 800-108 KBKDF (kbkdf_acvp_test, KDF 1.0 counter / feedback / double pipeline), ECDSA/EdDSA KeyVer (ecdsa_/eddsa_keyver_acvp_test, the key is used: sign with d, verify with the point), ECDSA SigGen verify-back (functional round-trip + independent oracle; NIST r, s are not reproducible because C_Sign draws its own nonce), EdDSA SigGen byte-match, the alternate-version SHA-2 MCT and full 100-iteration MCTs for SHA-2/SHA-3 and AES-CBC (sha_mct_full_test, aescbc_mct_full_test). Still open: ANSI X9.63 KDF on a caller-supplied Z — not expressible through PKCS #11 (declaredUnreachable x963-kdf-caller-z, gap x963-kdf-caller-z-no-mechanism); SHA LDT (1 GiB expanded message); HKDF multi-expansion groups; KBKDF with CMAC-TDES, HMAC-SHA2-512/224, /256 or bit-oriented output lengths; ECDSA/EdDSA KeyGen and deterministic ECDSA SigGen (no PKCS #11 input for the random / RFC 6979 nonce). The older HKDF/PBKDF2/X9.63/KBKDF workbench rows keep their published-standard / oracle / round-trip labels.
ANSI X9.63 KDF on a caller-supplied shared secret has no PKCS #11 mechanismaccepted-limitationunassignedEC++ + Rust (specification limit)—PKCS #11 v3.2 defines the X9.63 KDF only as the CK_EC_KDF_TYPE of CKM_ECDH1_DERIVE and related ECDH mechanisms (§6.3.16), applied to the shared secret the token derives from its own key pair; no mechanism accepts a caller-supplied Z, so the NIST kdf-components-ansix9.63-1.0 cases (Z, sharedInfo -> keyData) cannot be executed. Shown as the workbench skip row x963-kdf-unsupported and the capability-map declaredUnreachable row x963-kdf-caller-z. The KDF itself is exercised inside ECDH derivation (section 25, round-trip only).
C++: SP 800-108 KDFs ignore where the caller places the counter in the PRF input (FIXED IN REVIEW, hsm PR #275 — not yet on main)openunassignedEC++ (wasm) — fix in review (hsm #275), not on main—With the PKCS #11 v3.2 §6.42.3 / Table 199 data-parameter layout the NIST KDF 1.0 cases require, the C++ engine derives the wrong key whenever the counter is not in its fixed position: counter mode with the ITERATION_VARIABLE after or in the middle of the fixed data, and feedback / double-pipeline with a COUNTER before the chaining value or after the fixed data (kbkdf_acvp_test, 12 rows per run: HMAC-SHA2-256 and CMAC-AES128). 'Before fixed data' (and no counter) layouts byte-match NIST for every PRF. The engine appears to place the counter first (counter mode) or right after K(i-1)/A(i) (feedback / double pipeline) regardless of the CK_PRF_DATA_PARAM order. Engine defect — not patched in the hub. Citation tightened 2026-09-26: Table 199 (Counter Mode data field requirements) states CK_SP800_108_ITERATION_VARIABLE 'identifies the location of the iteration variable in the constructed PRF input data' and that 'the iteration variable for this KDF type is a counter' — the requirement is locational on its face, so this is a stated rule rather than an inference from the concatenation order. UPDATE 2026-09-26: a fix for all 12 placement cases is open as hsm PR #275 (44/0 across counter/feedback/double-pipeline × before-fixed/after-fixed/middle/before-iterator/none, with a negative control: forcing all 12 non-canonical placements through before-fixed-data ordering makes all 12 mismatch, so the probe discriminates; the 32 previously-passing cases still pass). The row stays OPEN deliberately because #275 is NOT merged — main still has this defect, and the shipped WASM bundle predates the fix. Two distinct causes, one symptom: counter mode (4 cases) was constrained by OpenSSL's KBKDF provider always writing the counter first, while feedback and double-pipeline (8 cases) were already hand-rolled on EVP_MAC by an earlier remediation and simply built [counter] || fixedInput unconditionally — our own hardcoding, not an OpenSSL limitation. The 56 vector cases are three groups, not two: 38 before-fixed-data positives, 12 placement, 6 counter-omission. 'before iterator' is a FOURTH placement (counter before the iteration variable K(i-1)/A(i)), accounting for 4 of the 12. CORPUS FINDING: the hsm repo's own tests/acvp/sp800_108_kbkdf_test.json and sp800_108_double_pipeline_test.json contain 42 cases that are ALL 'before fixed data' — the engine's own corpus exercised only the placement it already supported, which is why this never surfaced locally. The blind spot was the corpus, not only the code.
Rust: feedback and double-pipeline KBKDF reject the mandatory ITERATION_VARIABLE data parameteropenunassignedERust (wasm)—PKCS #11 v3.2 §6.42.4/§6.42.5 make CK_SP800_108_ITERATION_VARIABLE mandatory in feedback and double-pipeline mode (pValue NULL, ulValueLen 0; §6.42.2). The Rust engine returns CKR_MECHANISM_PARAM_INVALID for every such layout, so all 38 feedback / double-pipeline NIST KDF 1.0 rows fail. Probed 2026-09-25: without the ITERATION_VARIABLE entry the engine accepts the parameters and reproduces NIST except where the counter precedes the chaining value. Counter mode (ITERATION_VARIABLE with a counter format) is correct in every location. The hub keeps the specification layout; the existing sections 27/29 call the feedback mechanism without the ITERATION_VARIABLE. Engine defect — not patched in the hub.
Rust: C_CreateObject rejects a spec-complete RSA private key unless a non-standard CKA_VALUE is added — and our own import helper has been hiding itopenunassignedERust (wasm) + hub import helper (test fidelity)—PKCS #11 v3.2's RSA private key object table defines the key by CKA_MODULUS, CKA_PUBLIC_EXPONENT, CKA_PRIVATE_EXPONENT, CKA_PRIME_1/2, CKA_EXPONENT_1/2 and CKA_COEFFICIENT; it defines no CKA_VALUE. The Rust engine's import check (rust/src/ffi.rs, the per-class 'required key material' block, present on hsm origin/main 2026-09-26 and changed by none of the open PRs #272/#274/#275/#276/#277) gives RSA PUBLIC keys a branch accepting modulus + exponent, but RSA PRIVATE keys fall through to the final branch, which requires CKA_VALUE and otherwise returns CKR_TEMPLATE_INCOMPLETE (0xD0). Found 2026-09-26 by the hsm harness (tests/helpers.mjs importRSAPrivateKey, a complete eight-component CRT template, no CKA_VALUE) failing both RSA-OAEP unwrap KATs before OAEP was ever exercised. WHY OUR EVIDENCE NEVER SHOWED IT: src/wasm/softhsm.ts hsm_importRSAPrivateKey first tries the spec template PLUS a PKCS#8 DER blob in CKA_VALUE, commented 'Rust engine needs it', and only falls back to the spec template when C++ rejects the extra attribute. So every Rust RSA private-key import in the hub has gone through a non-standard template, and our RSA-OAEP decrypt row (rust 8/0) passed on a key a conformant caller could not have created. That pass is still a true statement about decryption, so the row is not narrowed; but it is NOT evidence that Rust accepts a standard RSA private-key import, and nothing in the matrix may be read that way. THE LESSON, the mirror image of kat-harness-must-read-stated-parameters: there, a harness assumption indicted a correct engine; here, a harness accommodation absolved a defective one. A workaround written into a test helper converts an engine defect into a silent pass. Remedy: engine fix (accept the CRT component set for CKO_PRIVATE_KEY/CKK_RSA), then remove the CKA_VALUE attempt from hsm_importRSAPrivateKey so the hub imports with the standard template on both engines. Engine defect — not patched in the hub. UPDATE 2026-09-26, later the same day. (1) ENGINE FIX IN REVIEW, NOT MERGED: the hsm session committed a15e5531 on fix/rust-rsa-private-component-import-0926 (branched from hsm main). As reported by that session, not re-measured here: C_CreateObject now accepts the v3.2 Table 38 component template, synthesises the internal PKCS#8 CKA_VALUE from it (so KMIP's register_rsa_private_key_pkcs8, which writes CKA_VALUE directly, is unaffected), checks supplied CRT values against p, q and d, and leaves a template that already carries CKA_VALUE untouched. (2) A SECOND, INDEPENDENT DIVERGENCE the fix exposed, also as reported and not re-measured here: once import succeeds, the Rust engine refuses RSA keys whose public exponent is 2^33 or more (CKR 0x13). The cause is deliberate DoS hardening in both Rust backends — the rsa crate (the only wasm backend) and aws-lc-rs (the primary native backend) — while OpenSSL (C++) accepts them, and FIPS 186-5 allows them. In NIST's own RSA-OAEP sample, 18 of the 20 keys have exponents of 34-56 bits. The hsm maintainer declined to lift the cap (patching rsa fixes wasm only; routing native keys through rsa brings RUSTSEC-2023-0071 onto the network-facing KMIP server), so it is pinned there as an executable expected failure: 2 small-exponent keys decrypt NIST's ciphertext end to end, 18 wide ones are refused, both counts fixed. (3) WHAT OUR ROW ACTUALLY RESTS ON, measured here: CKM_RSA_PKCS_OAEP|decrypt rust '8/0' is ONE decryption vector (rsa_oaep_test.json group 0 test 0: n 2048 bits, e = 65537, i.e. a 17-bit exponent) plus 7 error-path behaviour probes. It is true, and it is silent on wide-exponent keys: it must not be read as C++/Rust parity for RSA-OAEP. Recorded divergence: C++ accepts, Rust refuses, RSA public exponents >= 2^33. (4) OPEN DECISION: the hub import helper still sends the non-standard CKA_VALUE, and one standard-template-only Rust import test is still owed once a15e5531 merges. UPDATE 2026-09-26, measured here: the engine fix merged as hsm #278 (d1f74a52). rsa_oaep_test.json is now NIST's own KTS-IFC OAEP sample (tgId 1 SHA2-512 and tgId 3 SHA-1, 20 cases, independently decrypted before use), replacing the single Node-generated case. On it the C++ engine decrypts all 20; the Rust engine decrypts NONE. 18 keys are refused at import for public exponents >= 2^33, and the 2 small-exponent keys are both SHA-1 OAEP, which Rust refuses at C_DecryptInit with CKR_MECHANISM_PARAM_INVALID because every Rust OAEP path (rust/src/ffi.rs oaep_padding) supports SHA-256/384/512 only. Both refusals are pinned in src/wasm/rsaPrivateKeyImport.local.test.ts. SHA-1 OAEP is allowed by SP 800-56B rev 2 and PKCS #11 defines CKM_SHA_1/CKG_MGF1_SHA1 for it, so this is a second, separate Rust divergence, not a consequence of the exponent limit. Correcting an earlier relay in this row: the hsm session's report that 'the 2 small-exponent keys decrypt NIST's ciphertext end to end' does not hold on hsm main.
C++: invalid ECDSA / EdDSA public points are imported and used without validationopenunassignedEC++ (wasm) — off-curve and coordinate-range validation—The NIST keyVer cases with a point not on the curve or a coordinate out of range (ecdsa_keyver_acvp_test P-256/384/521, eddsa_keyver_acvp_test Ed25519/Ed448: 10 invalid points = 7 off-curve + 3 out-of-range, read from each case's `reason` field) are accepted by C_CreateObject and C_VerifyInit, and C_Verify then returns CKR_SIGNATURE_INVALID as for any wrong signature — no FIPS 186-5 / SP 800-56A public-key validation happens on import or use. BASIS (narrowed 2026-09-26 after a v3.2 review): an ACVP/FIPS and hardening defect, NOT a PKCS #11 v3.2 conformance violation. v3.2 does not require public-key validation — CKR_PUBLIC_KEY_INVALID appears exactly once and permissively ('may be returned by C_CreateObject … or by C_VerifyInit', lowercase 'may', and v3.2 makes keywords normative only in all capitals), and X9.62 is cited in the EC chapter only for parameter/point ENCODING, never as a validation mandate. The defect stands at full strength on its own ground: the ACVP KeyVer groups exist to test invalid-key rejection. SCOPE SPLIT 2026-09-26: the order/subgroup half of public-key validation is tracked separately as ec-subgroup-validation-ed25519-ed448 — and for the NIST PRIME curves it is not a gap at all, because SP 800-186 §3.2.1 gives P-224/256/384/521 cofactor h = 1 with prime order n, so no small-order or wrong-subgroup point exists and on-curve + coordinate range IS full validation there. Verified empirically: 3 independently-constructed on-curve points per curve, n·Q = O in 9 of 9.
Ed25519 / Ed448: small-order public points are not rejected, and NO official NIST vector exists to test itopenunassignedEC++ and Rust (wasm) — Ed25519 / Ed448 order check—Edwards25519 has cofactor h = 8 and Edwards448 h = 4 (SP 800-186 §3.2.3.1/§3.2.3.2: 'this curve has order h·n, where h = 8, and n is a prime number'), so small-order points genuinely EXIST on these curves — unlike the NIST prime curves, where h = 1 makes the order check mathematically redundant. A point that passes on-curve + coordinate-range and fails the order check is real and constructible: Ed25519 (0, p−1) has order 2 and (√−1, 0) has order 4; both satisfy the curve equation, both are in range, and for both L·T ≠ identity. NO OFFICIAL NIST VECTOR SET COVERS THIS, established 2026-09-26 from primary sources rather than from an absent search result: (1) ACVP-Server's EcdsaKeyDisposition.cs and EddsaKeyDisposition.cs enums are closed at 'none' / 'x or y out of range' / 'point not on curve' — no order member exists, so no generator can emit one, and an ACVTS-issued set buys nothing because the same code produces both sample and production vectors; the EDDSA KeyVer expectation provider emits only {None ×5, NotOnCurve ×5}. (2) The ACVP spec registers no invalidity-reason field for keyVer at all, and 'subgroup' appears zero times in the whole spec repo (positive control: 'partialVal' appears in 4 files). (3) KAS-ECC was the likeliest home and rules itself out in words — 'SP 800-56Ar3 - 5.6.2 Required Assurances. IUT assurance testing is outside the scope of ACVP testing' — and the generator's assurance-failure block is commented out in master; the three KAS-ECC reference sample sets carry only DKM/MacData/Z/Tag dispositions. (4) Legacy CAVP ecdsa/KeyVer/PKV.rsp has exactly three Result strings across all curves, including the binary curves that DO have cofactor > 1; the legacy KAS 'Validity Test' PKV failures were checked numerically over GF(2^283) and all 4 are off-curve (positive control: 22/22 pass-case points on-curve). (5) XECDH keyVer's reasons are encoding-level only ('too long', 'too short', 'most significant bit set'). CONSEQUENCE: this half cannot be closed by admitting a NIST vector, so any coverage must be self-derived and labelled as a distinct evidence class, not as a NIST-issued sample. Measured engine behaviour (reported by the v3.2 conformance-sweep session, 2026-09-26): OpenSSL does not validate Edwards point encodings at all — EVP_PKEY_public_check returns 1 (accepted) for an off-curve Ed25519 key, and EVP_DigestVerify returns 0 both for an off-curve key and for a valid key with a wrong signature, so the two are INDISTINGUISHABLE at the API. An attempt to add EVP_PKEY_public_check to OSSLEDPublicKey caught none of the 4 off-curve cases and intermittently broke DeriveTests::testEddsaDerive/X448 (1 of 2 runs; reverted). ENGINE EVIDENCE 2026-09-26 (Rust side, measured): Rust already rejects non-residues on the verify path because CompressedEdwardsY::decompress() returns Option, but nothing calls is_torsion_free, so small-order points still pass. So the exposure is confirmed in BOTH engines by reading, not inferred from the cofactor argument alone. Planned fixes use curve25519-dalek for Rust (already a direct dependency; is_torsion_free is the missing call) and libsodium for C++ (a new dependency requiring container, CMake, Emscripten and SBOM work, so the C++ half may land behind the Rust half). CORRECTION 2026-09-26 — THE SMALL-ORDER TABLE IS NOT SUFFICIENT, and an earlier note here implying it gave parity with curve25519-dalek's is_torsion_free was WRONG. A finite 4-row (Ed448) / 8-row (Ed25519) encoding table is `is_small_order`-equivalent, NOT `is_torsion_free`-equivalent. It does not reject a MIXED-ORDER point: Q = P + T where P is in the prime-order subgroup and T is a non-identity torsion point. Such a Q is on-curve, canonically encoded, and NOT small order, so it passes every table check — yet it lies outside the prime-order subgroup. Verified by construction on Ed448 rather than argued: taking an on-curve point, clearing the cofactor to get P in the prime-order subgroup, and adding the order-2 point (0, p-1) yields Q with on_curve(Q)=True, Q not in the small-order table, and L*Q != identity — while the same computation gives L*P == identity for the prime-order point and correctly flags the order-2 point as tabled. dalek's own documentation describes the three-way partition (small-order / mixed-order / prime-order) that the table collapses to two. THE CORRECT CHECK IS THE FULL PRIME-ORDER-SUBGROUP TEST, L*Q == identity, which is a real scalar multiplication. Maintainer ruling 2026-09-26: implement that in C++ (field arithmetic via OpenSSL BN_mod_*, so only the Edwards formulas and the ladder are ours), with THREE test classes rather than two — small-order points from the table, constructed MIXED-order points, and valid prime-order points as the negative control. The table remains useful as a cheap early reject and its values are proven correct for what they cover; it is simply not the whole check. Recorded because the table was briefly published here as sufficient, and a cheap-but-incomplete check that drives a vector count to zero while leaving the attack open is exactly the outcome this programme exists to prevent.
Rust: invalid public points are refused only at C_Verify, with a code C_Verify does not listopenunassignedE / G-8Rust (wasm)—The Rust engine imports the 10 invalid NIST keyVer points (C_CreateObject CKR_OK) and accepts C_VerifyInit, then C_Verify returns CKR_KEY_TYPE_INCONSISTENT — not among the §5.15.2 C_Verify return values. The keyVer rows pass (the point is refused with a key error), but validation belongs at C_CreateObject (§4.1.1 CKR_ATTRIBUTE_VALUE_INVALID) or C_VerifyInit.
Rust: Ed25519ph / Ed448ph signing drops the CK_EDDSA_PARAMS context (pre-hash path only)openunassignedERust (wasm)—C_Sign(CKM_EDDSA, phFlag = true, non-empty context) on the Rust engine produces the Ed25519ph / Ed448ph signature for an EMPTY context: all 8 preHash NIST EDDSA-SigGen-1.0 cases (contexts 96–248 bytes) differ from NIST, and @noble/curves ed25519ph verifies the Rust output without the context and rejects it with it. Pure Ed25519 / Ed448 with contexts byte-match. The C++ engine matches all 16 cases. Engine defect — not patched in the hub. SCOPE NARROWED 2026-09-26: this is NOT a general failure to honour pContextData. CKM_EDDSA already branches sign_eddsa vs sign_eddsa_ctx and handles the context correctly. The defect is confined to the pre-hash path — ffi.rs:7658 reads `CKM_EDDSA_PH => sign_eddsa_ph(&sk_bytes, eff_msg)` and passes no context argument at all. So: Ed25519ph/Ed448ph only, 8 cases. The previous title implied a broader defect than the code has. COUNT REVISED UPWARD 2026-09-26: 20 cases, not 8. The hub slice measured 8; the hsm repo's own tests/acvp/eddsa_test.json and eddsa_ed448_test.json hold 10 Ed25519ph + 10 Ed448ph NIST EDDSA-SigGen-1.0 pre-hash cases, every one with a non-empty context. FIX IN REVIEW (its own hsm PR, not yet on main): sign_eddsa_ph/verify_eddsa_ph now take the context as a REQUIRED parameter rather than an Option defaulting to None, so a new call site must decide rather than inherit a silent default. All 20 pass with byte-exact signature equality, not merely verify-accepts — the distinction matters because a sign/verify pair that drops the context on BOTH sides round-trips perfectly against itself while disagreeing with every conformant implementation (the same self-consistent-but-wrong shape as the CKM_ECDSA_SHA1 digest mismatch). Positive control: reverting only the sign path to the unconditional None fails on acvp-tc11, a 96-byte context. The test also asserts a modified context yields a different signature, that the original does NOT verify under the wrong context, and that at least one exercised vector had a non-empty context at all — without that last assertion an all-empty corpus would let a context-ignoring implementation pass everything.
Instrument scope mistaken for coverage: three corpus blind spots plus the reachability holes that hid themopenunassignedEValidation evidence model (both engines, hub and hsm corpora)—Our validation evidence has failed in three structurally different ways this programme, and they need different remedies. Recording them together because each was found only after a defect had already survived every green gate. (1) THE CORPUS CANNOT EXPRESS THE CASE. Our ACVP corpus is almost entirely positive-derivation: the oracle is 'does the output match'. A rejection rule — 'this invalid input MUST be refused' — has no way to be written down in it. Measured: all 56 kbkdf_acvp_test cases are positive, so PKCS#11 v3.2 Table 199's 'CK_SP800_108_COUNTER is invalid for this KDF type' is not merely untested but UNTESTABLE from this corpus. Remedy: acquire negative-polarity vectors (the SLH-DSA sigVer set landed 2026-09-26 brings 22 with testPassed:false across all six NIST reasons) or add product-mechanism probes. (2) THE CORPUS DOES NOT CONTAIN THE SHAPE. The hsm repo's own tests/acvp/sp800_108_kbkdf_test.json + sp800_108_double_pipeline_test.json hold 42 cases that are ALL 'before fixed data' — the one counter placement the engine already supported. A corpus containing only the shape you implement cannot find the shapes you did not. Remedy: acquire vectors covering the full parameter space. (3) THE VECTORS ARE LOADED BY A HARNESS NO GATE RUNS. CORRECTED 2026-09-26 — the first version of this row said the hsm repo's tests/acvp/eddsa_test.json and eddsa_ed448_test.json were referenced only by a COMMENT and that nothing read 'vectorSets'. THAT WAS WRONG, and the claim was published here before it was verified. Checked first-hand: tests/acvp-wasm.mjs:145-146 does `loadJson('eddsa_test.json')` / `loadJson('eddsa_ed448_test.json')`, and :1014 iterates `vecFile.vectorSets`, reaching every case. The 'only a comment' claim traces to a STALE line in docs/remediation-plan-pkcs11-v32-coverage-2026-08-29.md:374 that the repo's own later work superseded. Case counts were also wrong here: the real figures are eddsa_test.json 30 cases (14 with a non-empty context) and eddsa_ed448_test.json 31 cases (22 with a non-empty context) — not 20 and not 40; pre-hash is a vector-set property (scheme Ed25519ph/Ed448ph + phFlag), not a per-case key. THE REAL DEFECT IS ONE TIER REMOVED, and it belongs to the test-reachability class rather than this one: tests/acvp-wasm.mjs loads 43 of the 52 tests/acvp/*.json files, both eddsa files among them, and is declared as package.json 'test:acvp' — but `grep -rn 'acvp-wasm|test:acvp' .github/workflows/` returns ZERO hits. CI runs only ctest, cargo test, the Python replay harness and pytest. So the vectors were loaded by an existing harness that no gate executes. The blind-spot CLASS is real and the remedy is unchanged; only the example was mis-attributed. The hub's own instance of the same class: the four slhdsa_prehash_* files (172 cases on disk) are reached at HEAD only by testRegistry.ts, which is a DECLARATION, not a load — the loaders that execute them are uncommitted work in this worktree. MEASURED SCALE, once a reachability gate existed to ask the question: hub 0 live orphans (2 allowlisted with reasons) but 4 orphans/172 cases against the committed tree; hsm 106 orphaned vector files, including a 102-transcript kmip/kat/oasis-kmip-3.0 XML tree that is a byte-identical duplicate (diff -rq clean) of the kmip/conformance/oasis_corpus tree that IS replayed, plus lms_keygen_test.json + its expected file (240 tcIds) referenced only by prose. hsm already HAS an orphan guard (kmip/tests/acvp_roundtrip.rs::no_orphan_vector_files, a bidirectional CONSUMED/KNOWN_UNCONSUMED registry) but it is scoped to .json under kmip/kat/ only — it never looks at tests/acvp/ and deliberately skips XML, which is exactly how a 102-file duplicate tree stayed invisible. A guard's SCOPE is as load-bearing as its existence. THE GENERAL RULE stands: 'do we hold a vector for X', 'does anything load the vector for X' and 'does any gate run the thing that loads it' are THREE separate questions, and an affirmative to an earlier one has been read as an answer to a later one. Remedy: reachability gates at both tiers — vectors must be claimed by an executing test, and every test must be claimed by a gate. ROOT CAUSE IDENTIFIED 2026-09-26, and it unifies every instance above: INSTRUMENT SCOPE, NOT INSTRUMENT ABSENCE. In each case an instrument ran, passed or returned empty, and its result was read as a fact about the code when it was a fact about the instrument's own scope. Six instances in one day: (i) gate:pkcs11 scoped to Playground/* + src/wasm/softhsm reported a clean branch while 54 HPKE failures sat outside its paths; (ii) `npm run test:local -- <paths>` ran ZERO files and exited 0, because the local config's include matches only *.local.test.{ts,tsx}; (iii) `grep -i abi` over the validation data returned a reassuring result that was a false positive, matching inside 'cap-abi-lity'; (iv) a search over .rs/.cpp/.c/.py/.sh/.txt/.cmake concluded no loader existed for two vector files whose loader is a .mjs — the extension list excluded it, and the conclusion was published here as fact before being checked; (v) hsm's no_orphan_vector_files guard passed green while 102 duplicate XML transcripts sat un-owned, because that guard is scoped to .json under kmip/kat/ and deliberately skips XML; (vi) an OpenSSL version probe reported 3.5.7 by falling back to the system CLI while the engine links 3.6.3. THE GENERAL RULE: a guard's SCOPE is as load-bearing as its existence, and a passing guard is read as coverage of its whole category rather than of its actual scope — which makes a narrowly-scoped green guard more dangerous than no guard, because it actively reassures. REMEDIES, beyond the reachability gates: a command matching zero files must FAIL rather than exit 0; every negative claim needs a positive control on the same method; a guard must state its scope in its name, output and docs and REPORT out-of-scope items rather than skipping them silently; and prefer default-covered (inverse gate, fail on any unexempted new call site) over an allowlist, because an allowlist's omissions are silent. COUNT CORRECTION (measured, 2026-09-26): the hsm pre-hash corpus is 23 cases with 21 carrying a non-empty context — not the 20 earlier recorded; the two files hold 30 and 31 cases total (14 and 22 with non-empty contexts). It is NOT established whether that harness's assertions would have caught the context defect, and that gap is left visible rather than filled with a plausible sentence.
A KAT harness must READ a parameter the vector states, never assume it — and an ungated harness accumulates its own defectsopenunassignedEValidation evidence model + hsm harness (test fidelity)—Established 2026-09-26 by two harnesses disagreeing about the same mechanism through the same API, and resolved in favour of the one that read the vector rather than the one that assumed. THE INCIDENT: the hsm repo's tests/acvp-wasm.mjs reported 28 of 42 SP 800-108 failures against the Rust engine through C_DeriveKey, first described as all COUNTER mode, while our own sections/kdfDeriveAcvp.ts reported 19 pass / 0 fail for counter mode on the same engine through the same public API. (Not the same vector files: the hsm harness uses per-mode files — sp800_108_counter_test.json, sp800_108_double_pipeline_test.json and a feedback file, 14 tests each, every group 'before fixed data' — while we use kbkdf_acvp_test.json, 56 groups spanning every counterLocation.) One of them had to be wrong. FIRST HYPOTHESIS, MINE, AND WRONG: that the harness marshalled CK_ULONG as 8 bytes when wasm32 makes it 4, misaligning every field after the first. Falsified by direct comparison: both harnesses allocate 12 bytes per CK_PRF_DATA_PARAM and write every field as i32, and both use a 20-byte params struct with i32 fields — byte-for-byte equivalent and correct. ACTUAL CAUSE: our implementation DERIVES the CK_PRF_DATA_PARAM order from each vector group's own `counterLocation` field (kdfDeriveAcvp.ts:261-277 reads g.counterLocation and orders [iterationVariable, data] for 'before fixed data', [data, iterationVariable] for 'after fixed data', and so on). The hsm harness HARDCODES the order — and hardcodes it inconsistently: its counter helper emits BYTE_ARRAY then ITERATION_VARIABLE (counter AFTER fixed data) while its feedback helper in the same file emits ITERATION_VARIABLE, COUNTER, BYTE_ARRAY (counter BEFORE fixed data). Every vector in that corpus is 'before fixed data', and the section comment says so. So the counter helper intended one layout and constructed the opposite. CORRECTED LATER THE SAME DAY: the 28 were 14 counter + 14 DOUBLE-PIPELINE, not 28 counter — the count had been generalised from the first screen of failures (the peer's own correction). Swapping the counter helper's order took counter failures 14 -> 0 (overall 277/33 -> 291/19). The remaining 14 double-pipeline failures have THE SAME CAUSE, verified by reading the code: sp800108DoublePipelineKdf's docstring says 'A(i) || counter || fixedInput — same before-fixed-data placement' but the code emits ITERATION_VARIABLE, BYTE_ARRAY, OPTIONAL_COUNTER, i.e. counter AFTER fixed data, against a 14-test corpus that is 100% 'before fixed data'. Of the three helpers, feedback is the only one whose code matches its own comment — and it is the only mode that passed. (Predicted, not yet measured: swapping that helper takes double-pipeline 14 -> 0.) These 14 are NOT our matrix's Rust double-pipeline 0/18: ours are measured on main's Rust, which rejects every layout containing ITERATION_VARIABLE (rust-kbkdf-iteration-variable-rejected), whereas the peer's Rust build passes feedback and so already carries the hsm PR #277 fix for that rejection. Different build, different cause, both recorded. The ENGINE IS CORRECT: Rust honours segment order, emitted fixed||counter as instructed, and mismatched an expected value computed as counter||fixed. THE PRINCIPLE, which is the durable finding: IF A VECTOR FILE STATES A PARAMETER, THE HARNESS MUST READ IT RATHER THAN ASSUME IT. A hardcoded assumption about input shape encodes a second, invisible expectation that nothing checks — and when it is wrong, it indicts the implementation under test. The test that derived its input shape from the vector's own metadata was right; the test that hardcoded it was wrong. WHY IT SURVIVED, and this compounds an earlier finding: that corpus is entirely 'before fixed data', so a hardcoded order was never contradicted by the data. Same corpus-shape blind spot logged earlier today, now biting the TEST CODE rather than the engine. AND THE STRONGEST CONSEQUENCE: this is the first instrument-scope instance found INSIDE test code rather than in a search, a gate or a guard. A harness that no gate executes does not merely fail to catch defects — IT ACCUMULATES ITS OWN, because nothing ever contradicts it. tests/acvp-wasm.mjs is invoked by no workflow, and it had drifted into asserting the wrong thing. That is a stronger argument for gating it than any coverage argument. EFFECT ON OUR CLAIMS: none — no narrowing needed. The matrix rows for CKM_SP800_108_COUNTER_KDF (rust 19/0), FEEDBACK (1/20) and DOUBLE_PIPELINE (rust 0/18, cpp 14/4) stand as measured. The C++ double-pipeline 4 fails are exactly the 'before iterator' and 'after fixed data' groups (tg 5083, 5068, 4472, 4456) — the genuine placement defect cpp-kbkdf-counter-position-ignored (fixed in review, hsm PR #275); C++ passes all 12 'before fixed data' and both 'none' groups. A CORPUS CONSEQUENCE WORTH NAMING: a before-fixed-data-only corpus cannot detect that C++ defect at all, and the hsm harness's wrong layout would likely cancel it — the engine places the counter right after A(i) regardless of the order it is given, which is where the vector wants it. Two defects that cancel look like a pass. RESIDUAL EVIDENCE-MODEL LIMITATION, recorded because it is real and unfixed: the coverage matrix presents ONE verdict per mechanism per engine and has no way to express that two independent tests of the same mechanism through the same API disagree. Here the disagreement was resolved, but the matrix could not have shown it.

The hsm gate builds the WASM engine with a 2 MiB stack while the shipped artifact uses 8 MiB — it validates a configuration nobody shipsopenunassignedEpqctoday-hsm gate configuration (test fidelity; nothing shipped affected)—Measured 2026-09-26, verified first-hand in pqctoday-hsm: `rust/build-wasm-bundle.sh:39` — the script that produces the SHIPPED artifact the hub vendors — sets `-C link-arg=-zstack-size=8388608` (8 MiB), and `scripts/build-kmip-wasm.sh:39` sets the same. But `scripts/local-gate.sh:590` builds the engine for the gate with `RUSTFLAGS='-C link-arg=-zstack-size=2097152'` (2 MiB). So the gate exercises a link configuration that is never shipped. WHY IT MATTERS: at 2 MiB, every SLH-DSA parameter set above 128 dies with 'memory access out of bounds' — which is how a stack overflow presents in WASM, the shadow stack pointer walking out of its region. On the 2 MiB build the ACVP harness reports 40 PASS / 190 FAIL; on an 8 MiB build the same harness reports 277 PASS / 33 FAIL / 12 SKIP. So a gate red of 190 cases is a single link-flag artefact of the gate's own configuration, and none of it reflects the shipped artifact. CLASSIFICATION: a TEST-FIDELITY defect, not a product defect. Nothing shipped is affected and no frozen evidence was ever built at 2 MiB. Recorded because the divergence cuts both ways — a gate that builds a different configuration can raise a false alarm (as here) and can equally miss a real defect that only the shipped flags expose. PROVENANCE OF THIS ROW, recorded because it is itself an instance of the pattern: this was first reported to us as a CRASHING SHIPPED BUNDLE and an urgent block on the WASM rebuild, on the assumption that the 2 MiB flag found in local-gate.sh governed the bundle — without checking that a DIFFERENT script builds the shipped artifact. That claim was relayed onward before being verified, and retracted after `grep -n stack-size` across both scripts showed 8388608 in the builder and 2097152 only in the gate. Fifth instrument-scope error of the day: the instrument (the flag that was read) measured something adjacent to the subject (the flag that ships). Same family as `gate:pkcs11`'s path scope hiding 54 failures, a grep whose extension list excluded the loader, an orphan guard scoped to one directory and file type, and a version probe that read the system CLI instead of the linked library. See gap `vector-corpus-blind-spots` for the unified root cause. ALSO FOUND, and more useful than the flag itself: `tests/acvp-wasm.mjs` has NO test isolation, so one module fault renders a single bug indistinguishable from mass failure — 185 identical failure detail strings, first failure at output line 84, last pass at line 82, zero passes after. Without per-section isolation a fixable one-line build flag reads as a catastrophe, which trains readers to discount the harness. Any gate over that harness needs isolation in the same change. AND: that harness reported `0 PASS / 0 FAIL / 80 SKIP` for HSS SHAKE-256 while the suite looked green — 80 NIST cases contributing nothing. A skip is an untested case wearing a pass's colour. The hsm maintainer has ruled that no skip is acceptable without review; all 50 skip sites are being enumerated with real causes. When that enumeration arrives it must be cross-checked against our coverage matrix, because a case that harness skips while our matrix counts it covered is a live over-claim on our side.
